AMERIGAN CONSULATE
Nagoya, Japan.
Oetober 11 1955
September 4, 1935
new
note
894.607
SUBJECT: Nagoya Pan Pacific Peace Exhibition
1937
1—1055
eFO
894.607
2A
10 1935
THE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE
WASHINGTON
NAGOYA/I
I have the honor to transmit under separate cover
twelve copies of a pamphlet in English regarding an
international exhibition, to be called the Nagoya Pan
Paeiric Peace Exhibition, which is to be held in Nagoya
from March 15 to May 31, 1937, under the auspices of
the City of Nagoya approved and supported by the Aichi
Prefecture and the Nagoya Chamber of Commerce and
Industry. Twelve copies of an additional pamphlet,
"Guide to Nagoya, 1935", 1ssued by the City of Nagoya,
OCT 14 1935
FILED
are also tramsmitted.
The good offices of this Consulate have been en-
listed by the Municipality of Nagoya to bring the
projected exhibition to the attention of my Government
and to solicit its active participation.
At a preliminary (banquet) meeting at the Municipal
Auditorium recently which I was invited to attend, ad-
dresses on the aims and purposes of and plans for the
exhibition were made by the Mayor and the President of
the Chamber of
F/G
CVE  LOW FILES.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
the Chamber of Commerce, following which I was un-
expectedly called upon for a few remarks or comments.
Briefly, in substance, I thanked the Mayor for the
honor of the invitation (I was the only foreigner
present), and for the honor of being called upon to
address the meeting; that I could but congratulate the
City on its worthy efforts to bring the nations of the
Pacific together in a Peace Exhibition, which I felt
would meet with the hearty approval of my Government
in particular and of my people in general; and that I
would be glad to transmit officially to the Department
of State at Washington the invitation of the City of
Nagoya for the United States to take an active part in
the exhibition, and would personally be happy to lend
every possible assistance to help make the exhibition
a suocess. I took the occasion also to congratulate
the City on its great forward strides in modern city
planning and improvements; its fine well lighted wide
streets; its many and increasing number of modern
buildings, and excellent transportation; as well as
on its increasing importance in commerce and industry
and foreign trade and shipping, all of which would un-
doubtedly be further enhanced upon the completion of
the vast harbor ertension work projected and under
construotion, and by the opening nert year of the new
twenty-five million yen Nagoya Station, which, I under-
stood, would be the largest and finest in the country.
I added that the projected new Kanko Hotel, designed
for foreign tourists, to be completed by the end of
l9s6, would be a further important step in the
forward strides

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
forward strides of the Central Capital, third largest
city in the Empire; and concluded that I believed
that all this, and not to mention the many historic
and scenic attractions of the city and the nearby
surroundings - such as the famous Castle and the
Atsuta Shrine, the magnificent Municipal Office Build-
ing, the modern porcelain and textile factories;
Nagaragawa with its cormorant fishing, Inuyama Castle
and the Japan Rhine, and the Gamagori seaside resort
and its excellent hotel - would all lend attraction to
foreign visitors, many of whom would surely take
advantage of the opportunity to visit here during the
exhibition. I could not help but feel therefore that
the exhibition would be a great success and add materially
to the promotion of Peace in the Pacific.
Having spoken in English, my remarks were trans-
lated into Japanese by my Honorary Argentine colleague,
Mr. Mampei Abe.
The City hopes that the United States will take an
active part in the exhibition, which will be the first
international exhibition ever held in Japan and the
largest exhibition ever held in the Empire.
It is understood that the Foreign Office has been
asked to approach our Government soliciting participa-
tion and every possible assistance, and my good offices
have been sought to the end that the exhibition may be
favored with the erection of a representative American
building to house representative exhibits and other
attractions.

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
other attractions.
Plans of the city and in particular of the large
reclaimed land area in the southern part of the city
near the port, where the exhibition is to be located,
are now being printed, together with other material in
English, for the benefit of foreign governments and
exhibitors, and copies will be transmitted as soon as
they may be received.
Building space, as large as may be desired, it is
understood, would be provided without rental charge,
while the cost of the construction of the building, of
course, would have to be borne by the United States or
the representative American exhibitors who would use it.
Arrangements for special freight rates for exhibits,
as well as for free customs entry, it is understood will
also be made.
The wishes of the Nagoya Municipality and the pro-
motors of the exhibition are accordingly brought to the
Department's attention for such action as may seem
appropriate.
Copies of this despatch with its enclosureswill
be sent to the Embassy, Consulate General and Commercial
Attache at Tokyo.
Respectfully yours,
24 encoc
American Vice Consul
Under separate cover:
24 pamphlets.

9-21-35

Translation by the American Embassy, Tokyo, of a note
in Japanese received on September 19, 1935.
h:g
No. 18 Go, Communications III.
Department of Foreign Affairs,
Tokyo, September 16, 1935.
Sir:
I have the honor to inform you that the city of
Nagoya, in celebration of the thirtieth anniversary of the
opening of the city port to international trade, is to hold
a Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exposition from March 15, 1937,
to May 31, 1937, in the interest of industry and culture
at home and abroad as well as of peace, good relations and
the mutual prosperity of nations. I am approached by the
promoters of the exhibition with a request to use my good
offices for the perticipation of the countries bordering the
Pacific, India, and other countries having elose relations
with Nagoya. I shall be grateful if you will urge the United
States.of America to participate.
In view of the fact that the reservation of space for
exhibits is considered necessary, I beg you will kinaly inform
me, at as earlya date as possible, of the attitude of the
United States as to participation.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to you the
assurances of my highest consideration.
Koki Hirota(seal)
Edwin L. Neville,Esquire,
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim
of the United States of America.

Dear Mr. Moore:
894.607 NAG0YA/12
Replying to your letter of October 11, PC 894.607-Nagoya/1,
this Department will be interested in participating in & Government
exhibit at the Nagoya Pan Pacific Peace Exhibiticn in the event that
such participation is authorized by the Congress. The importance
of Nagoya as a manufacturing center, and the market in Japan for
our agricultur l products, partic larly cotton, makes such parti-
cipation desirable. If it is finally decided that the Federal
Government will not participate in this Exhibition then we shall be
glad to cooperate with the Department of State and the Department of
Commerce in bringing this exposition to the attention of commercial
agencies interested in export trade, including cooper tive marketing
associations.
Sincerely,
mewils
Acting Secretary.
NOV 19 1935.

MEMORANDUM
The ttached informal memorandum, to-
gether with copies of pamphlets relating to
the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition, were
left with me today by Mr. Hayama of the
Japanese Embassy.
Mr. Hayama stated that he was aware'
that the Foreign Office at Tokyo had
894.60
officially communicated with the American
Embassy in regard to possible American
participation in the Exhibition, but that
the Japanese Embassy at Washington was
independently bringing to the attention of
the Department the plans for the holding of
the Bxhibition, in the hope that it would
be possible for the American Government to
participate therein.
I informed Mr. Hayama that, upon
receipt of a report from the American
Embassy at Tokyo, the Department took steps
to bring the request of the Japanese Foreign
Office to the attention of the appropriate
Departments of this Government.

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.98)
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
JAPANESE EMBASSY
NOV 1 5 1935
#
WASHINGTON
Department of State
1.
With a view to rendering service to the
development of the peace and cul ture of the
Pan-Pacific countries, the City of Nagoya,
894.607 NAG0YA/18
Japan, has planned to hold a grand Pan-Pacific
Exhibition from March 15 to May 31. 1937 with
the help of the Nagoya Chamber of Commerce and
Industry and the Aichi Prefectural Office repre-
senting the Nagoya district. Full particulars
are contained in the annexed pamphlet. The
chief ambition is to display the present and
past situations of the industry and culture of
all coastal states and to contribute to the amity
and friendship as well as the joint progress and
prosperity of the interested nations. However,
no such extensive and comprehensive enterprise
could be accomplished without the full support
and assistance of all the Governments, public
bodies and the peoples themselves of all the
states and countries concerned.
2.
Under these circumstances, the Department
of Foreign Affairs of Japan has already asked
F/FG
the

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.98)
the interested foreign and diplomatic envoys in
Tokio to use their good offices in securing a
wide participation in the Exhibition.
3.
The Japanese Government earnestly hope
that the United States Government will under-
stand well the purport of the Exhibit ion, and
will lend its efforts to enable them to fulfil
the ardent desire and purpose of promoting the
peace and progress of all humanity.

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 159 of December 12, 1935
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
No. 491.
Embassy of the
United States of America.
Excellency:
Tokyo, December 7, 1935.
I have the honor to refer to Your Excellency's com-
munication of September 16, 1935, No. 18 Go, Communications
III, informing me that the city of Nagoya, in celebration
of the thirtieth anniversary of the opening of the city
port to international trade, is to hold a Nagoya Pan-Pacific
Peace Exposition from March 15, 1937 to May 31, 1937, in
the interest of industry and culture at home and abroad as
well as of peace, good relations, and the mutual prosperity
of nations. Your Excellency requests that the participa-
tion of the United States be urged and asks to be informed,
at as early a date as possible, of the attitude of the
United States as to participation.
The matter having been referred to my Government I now
have the honor to state that I am instructed to inform Your
Excellency that it is not probable that the Government of the
United States will be able officially to participate in the
Exposition but that the appropriate Departments of the American
Government are being requested to give publicity to the
Exposition.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to Your
Excellency the assurances of my highest consideration.
Edwin L. Neville.
His Excellency
Mr. Koki Hirota,
His Imperial Japanese Majesty's
Minister for Foreign Affairs,
eto.,
etc.,

January 15, 1936.
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
SUBJECT:
Inauguration of Prince Higashi Kuni
1--1055
ero
as Governor of the Pan Pacific Peace
894.607-NAG0YA/24
Exhibition
THE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE
WASHINGTON
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Consulate's
despatch No. 22 of September 4, 1935, and to.the
Department's instruction (File No. 894.607 - Nagoya/1)
dated October 1l, 1935, concerning the Pan Pacific
Peace Exhibition which is to be held at Nagoya from
March 15 to May 31, 1937.
Reference is also made to the Department's
FEB 20 936
FTLED
instruction (No. 877) to the Embassy at Tokyo, dated
November 1l, l935, referring to the text of a note
from the Foreign Minister transmitting an invitation
on behalf of the promoters of the Exhibition, and
stating that "as there is no indication that the
Japanese Government itself intends participating in
this Exposition, it is thought that this communication
may not properly be considered an official invitation
on the part of the Japanese Government".
Seemingly to overcome this impression and with a
view no doubt to obtain the active participation of
the United States
F/G

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the United States Government, as well as that of other
Governments bordering on the Pacific, Prince Higashi
Kuni, at a formal ceremony at the Municipal Auditorium,
was today inaugurated as Governor of the forthcoming
Exhibition.
Personally present on this occasion also were
Mr. Machida, Minister of Commerce and Industry, and
a representative of the Foreign Office, both of whom,
following the Prince's brief acceptance speech, de-
livered formal addresses announcing the Japanese
Government's approval, support and active partici-
pation in the Exhibition.
Following the inauguration ceremony and to cele-
brate the occasion, Prince Higashi Kuni acted as host
at a luncheon to about 600 persons including numerous
officials and other prominent persons and the leading
business men and bankers of Nagoya.
I was granted an audience by the Prince in which
I expressed pleasure on his acceptance of the Governor-
ship of the Exhibition.
It is understood that the Prince is very interested
in the commercial and industrial development of Nagoya,
having been assigned with the Army here for about three
years.
Copies of this despatch will be sent to the
Embassy, Consulate General and Commercial Attache' at
Tokyo for their information.
Respectfully yours,
Iharler S Stephan
Charles H. Stephan,
American Vice Consul.

March 10, 1936

Assistant Secretary of State,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.
894.607 NAG0YA/27
Dear Mr. More:
UI
STATE
The Secretary has asked me to reply to your
letter of March 6 with which you enclosed three copies
of the circular, Directions to Exhibitors, transmitted
by the American Vice Consul at Nagoya, Japan in connection
with the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition.
While our contacts with agencies likely to be
interested in this Exposition are much more limited than
those of the Department of Commerce, we shall be glad to
do what we can to give publicity to it.
Yours very truly,
C
rewarbrton
C. W. Warburton,
Director of Extension Work.

Enclosure No. l to despatoh
No.1813 ofMay 7, 1936 from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
COPY
THE NAGOYA PAN-PACIFIC PEACE EXHIBITION
Nagoya, April 27th, 1936.
His Excellency Joseph Clark Grew,
Ambassador Extraordinary and Plenipotentiary,
American Embassy,
1, Enokizaka-machi, Azabu-ku.
Tokyo.
Your Excellency,
Since the receipt of a notification from the Gaimu-
sho under the date of December 17, 1935, that the United
States of America was not in a position to participate
as Government in the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition,
we have been hesitating to approach Your Excellency again
in this connection. We are, however, too eager to give
up the hope of acquiring means of arriving at some satis-
factory conclusion of this matter, and please excuse us
for our troubling Your Excellency once more.
The projected Exhibition, as was repeatedly explained,
aims at the promotion of the world-wide peace and welfare
of the nations surrounding the Pacific Ocean and also of
those in close contact with our city. With this lofty
ideal in view, we have been under the impression that the
full participation from your Government and from various
quarters of American industrialists and business men under
the sole guidance of your Government will surely be of the
most important constituents of our xhibition.
We might mention that your country is one of the best
customers of our City and in l935 we exported a total value
of ¥22,7u0,000 including porcelain, toys, cotton tex-
tiles, etc. and the import from your country to our city
through the vagoya harbour is valued at only 6,500,000.
But the raw cotton absorbed in Nagoya and its vicinity
last year was 171,452,970 Kg., value ¥97,898,833. The
greater part of this immense quantity, we have no doubt
from its quality, is imported from your country through
other ports. In addition to this the import of timber,
rayon, pulp, machinery, etc., to this city has the ten-
dency of gradual increase year after year, and further
increase in future will be well anticipated, keeping step
with the rapidindustrial progress of our City.
At this juncture of the opening of the Nagoya Pan-
Pacific Peace Exhibition, we deem it our great mission
and pleasant duty to bring the most brilliant and advanced
culture of your country to our peoples' notice and to deepen
their

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
their actual knowledge about your country, so that the
intimaoy and friendly relations of these two neighboring
countries across the Pacific will be further cemented,
and that our mutual economical relations may, thus,be
greatly promoted.
We feel it very grateful that your Government will
take trouble to give our Exhibition every publicity to
all the authorities concerned, even though your Govern-
ment can not participate as Government. We are now doing
our very best for taking the matter up direct with your
various cities coastaling along the Pacific and also
other cotton producing districts such as Dalas, etc.
Under such circumstances, we feel very sorry that we
have to trouble Your Excellency repeatedly, but we
shall feel very much obliged if Your Excellency will
specially reconsider the matter and kindly see if there
is any means of acquiring participation from your Govern-
ment, or in case this is impossible, any other alternative
means that will gather thorough going participation
from your country.
Availing ourselves of this opportunity to assure
Your Excellency of our highest respects,
We are, Sir,
Your Excellency's obedient servant,
(Signed) Isao Oiwa.
Mayor of Nagoya, and Government
of the Nagoya Pan-Pacifio Peace
Exhibition.

No.1813 ofMay 7, 1936 from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
C O P Y
American Embassy
Tokyo, May 7, 1936.
Sir:
The Embassy has received and read with interest
your letter of April 27, in further reference to the
participation by the United States in the Nagoya Pan-
Pacific Peace Exhibition and will forward a copy to
Washington for consideration.
Accept, Sir, the assurances of my distinguished
consideration.
Joseph C. Grew
Ambassador.
His Honor
Isao Oiwa,
Mayor of Nagoya.

Rental Rates tor Exhibit Space
A. Of the E hibits from abroad. only those furnished by
foreign Government, States cities and other publie bodies,

shall be defraved by the Eshibitors (Art. 2., Rules and
Regulations for Foreign Exhibits of the Nagoya Pan-Pacific
Peace Exhibition)
B. All the Exhibits. except those specially mentioned
above, shall be charged the following rates for their alloted
Exhibition spaces: 
I) Ground rents for Special Building:
a) Special Grade-Y50 for 6 feet square (some 1.
8 meters square).
b) First Grade—Y40 for the same space
II.) Rents for Spaces in the Exhibition-halls:
a) Special Grade - Y60 for 6 feet square (some 1.
8 meters square).
b) First Grade—Y50 for the same space.
III.) Rents for Spaces with Show-cases in the Exhibition-
halls:
a) Special Grade 65 for 6 feet wide and 3 feet
deep (some 1.8 m ters wide and some0.9 meters deep).
b) First Grade-Y60 for the sane space.
IV.) Rents for Spaces with Show-stands in the Exhibition
halls:

deep (some 1.8 meters wide and some 0.9 meters deep).

above shall be specially arranged whenever necessary.
(Art. 3., Rules and Regulations for Foreign Exhibits of the

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
ALL ABROAD INTERESTED IN JAPAN.
THE MOST ELABORATE INTERNATIONAL
EXHIBITION THAT JAPAN HAS EVER KNOWN
TO BEHELD
IN THE CTTY OF NAGOYA.
Situated in the central part of Japan, the City of Nagoya
is now one of the most important industrial cities of the
country, with a population of over a million and a history
of time honoured culture, and it is meant to commemorate
the City's recent advances in commerce and industry.
The contents of the Exhibition are rich in variety and
choice of quality, local colour having been taken into full
consideration. In addition to the smiling blossoms of the
season, the Exhibition is a never-failing lure to visitors to
Japan.
Prospective tourist in Europe, America and all over the
world are advised to travel in 1937 to Japan to include the
Exhibition in Nagoya, which will be a comprehensive survey
of Japanese civilization as well as foreign.

Sir:
I have the honor to report that a Mr. Kurow Itoh,
a Japanese, representative of several Japanese firms and
special representative of the Mayor of Nagoya, Japan,
arrived by airplane in Bogotá on October 7, l936 for the
stated purpose of requesting Colombian participation in
a Pan-Pacific Peace Exposition to be held at Nagoya, and
transmitting an official invitation from the Japanese
3
Government to that effect.
FILED
In a concise interview with the reporter of EL
2
193t
TIEMPO, Mr. Kurow Itoh stated that the occasion of the
exposition was the termination of certain municipal
improvements at Nagoya, principal of which was a 37 million
yen
F/FG

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
yen port project. He believes it advantageous for
Colombia to send exhibits and commercial representatives
to Japan since only ten such Colombian representatives
had visited Japan during the past ten years, principally
buyers, while Japan had sent more than one hundred and
fifty "travelling advertisers" to Colombia during the
past ten years. According to Mr. Itoh, the United States,
Mexico, Guatemala, Salvador, Honduras, Costa Rica, Panama,
and Venezuela, have agreed to participate in the
exposition.
Mr. Itoh also stated that despite the tea-drinking
proclivities of the Japanese people, the value of
Japanese annual coffee im orts has increased from five
thousand yen 30 years ago, to five million yen per year
at present. He suggested that if one-tenth of the
Japanese population of eighty millions could be induced
to consume a cup of coffee a day, a project which might
conceivably be realized within ten years, the Japanese
market would consume approximately one-half of Colombian
coffee production.
The Nagoya exposition is evidently to be held during
the summer of 1937, and Mr. Itoh is negotiating for the
transfer of exhibits to Japan from the Barranquilla,
Colombia, exhibition to be opened at that city during
December 1936. No official decision concerning participa-
tion in the Japanese exposition has been announced as yet
by the Colombian Government.
Respectfully

at Nagoya. The Minister is reported to have informed

I have the honor to submit the following report
in regard to the opening to the public today, March
15, 1937, of the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition,
which is to remain open until May 3l, 1937.
Before some 15,000 specially invited guests,
including many high Government officials and repre-
sentatives of foreign governments, the Mayor of Nagoya,
a Governor of the Exhibition, in the presence of
48
PWince Higashi Kuni, patron and Honorary President
of the Exhibition, held a formal opening ceremony at
the Exhibition Grounds, situated in the south section
of the city near the port, on Sunday afternoon, March
AH 80 1837
FU.
14, 1937.
Among the principal guests present, or repre-
sented by proxies, were the following:
Prime Minister, represented by Mr. Adachi, Vice
Governor of Aichi Prefecture.
Minister of Commerce and Industry, represented
by Mr. Azuma, of the Bureau of Commerce.
Minister of Foreign Affairs, represented by Mr.
Yamagata.
Minister of Navy, represented by Captain Kondo.
General Ito, representing the Third Army Division,
F/FG
Nagoya, and other military officials.
Chief Judges

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Chief Judges of the Nagoya District Court and
the Nagoya Court of Appeals.
Director of the Nagoya Railway Bureau.
Director of the Nagoya Bureau of Communications.
The Governors of Aichi, Gifu and Shiga Prefec-
tures.
Deans of the Commercial College, the Technical
College and the Eighth Higher School.
The Vice Governor of Hyogo Prefecture.
Members of the House of Representatives.
Minister for The Netherlands.
American Ambassador, represented by the American
Vice Consul at Nagoya.
French Ambassador, represented by a Secretary
of the Embassy.
British Consul General at Osaka, possibly repre-
senting the British Ambassador and the
Canadian Minister.
Netherlands Consul at Kobe.
Netherlands Indies Government official repre-
sentatives.
Australian Trade Commissioner.
South African Trade Commissioner for the Far
East, from Batavia.
Chinese Vice Consul at Nagoya.
The guests were seated in the open stands and
in the huge open-air circus arena, shown as area No.
46 on the accompanying plan of the Exhibition Grounds.
At the north end of this area a large canvas-covered
pavilion was erected for Prince Higashi Kuni, and for
the most prominent Japanese and foreign guests and
speakers. Numerous other important Japanese and
foreign guests were seated in specially assigned
places in canvas-covered areas on the ground immedi-
ately adjoining and on either side of the pavilion.
The speakers' tables were equipped with microphones
for amplification of their voices throughout the
arena, and the whole was festively decorated in
flowers and bunting and flags of all countries.
Preceding the arrival of Prince Higashi Kuni, army
and navy aeroplanes swooped down low over the arena,
dropping colored leaflets.
Prince

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Prinoe Higashi Kuni entered under the guidance
of the Mayor during the rendition of the national
anthem , the "Kimigayo", by a military band; and
while all present remained standing, the Mayor of
Nagoya, addressing his remarks to Prince Higashi Kuni,
announced the opening of the Exhibition and read a
resume' report in regard thereto.
He then briefly
reviewed the progress of the development of Nagoya
since the establishment of the municipal administra-
tion 49 years ago, with particular emphasis on the
great strides which have been made in more recent
years, making of the city a prosperous and flourish-
ing modern international port and commercial and
industrial metropolis of major importance in Japan.
Continuing, the Mayor added that, to mark the com-
pletion of this present stage of the development of
Nagoya, the Exhibition was designed to bring together
in friendly intercourse people from all over the
world, and particularly those of countries bordering
on the Pacific, for the promotion of peace in this
world area, through the display of their cultural,
industrial and commercial exhibits. Concluding, the
Mayor briefly sketched the scope of the Exhibition,
stating that there are 38 particular areas devoted to
exhibits from 30 foreign countries; that there are
86 buildings, including the Hall of Industry and the
Main Hall; that there are 7 Special Foreign Halls
and 23 other Special Halls; that the total building
area comprises 20,515 tsubo (164 acres); and that
there are 1,063 organizations and 3,257 individual
exhibitors.
Replying to

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
Replying to the Mayor's address, Prinoe Higashi
Kuni read a message the gist of which was as follows:
As the world's civilization has progressed
and communications have expanded, the country's
urgent obligation is to improve international
relations and extend economic intercourse. The
Exhibition is supported by Pacific coast
countries and others, and its purpose is to
introduce the country's present state of civil-
ization to them and to promote world peace.
Concluding, the Prince stated that the
present Exhibition shows a vast improvement
over former exhibitions [in Japan], but that,
to keep abreast of rapid world progress, continued
earnest efforts must be expended.
Thereafter congratulatory addresses of the Prime
Minister, the Minister of Commerce and Industry and
the Minister for Foreign Affairs, were read by official
representatives of those ministries. Following these
the Minister for the Netherlands delivered a con-
gratulatory address, in French, on behalf of his
country only. It was apparently the intention of
the officials in charge of the program that the senior
foreign representative present would speak on behalf
of all foreign representatives. However, the Nether-
lands Minister was seemingly given no opportunity to
confer or consult with any other foreign representa-
tives, so that he spoke only for his own country
which, through

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
which, through the Government of The Netherlands
Indies, is taking a very active part in the Exhibition
with an extensive exhibit from Java.
A maval representative next read an address, and
he was followed by the Governor of Aichi Prefecture,
and the President of the Supporters' Association of
the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition, and a repre-
sentative of the Exhibitors' Association.
Thereafter a few of numerous congratulatory
telegrams were read, following which the Mayor, again
addressing his remarks to Prince Higashi Kuni, announced
the ceremony closed.
The Prince retired during the
playing of the "Kimigayo", following which many of the
guests inspected the grounds and the exhibits, so far
as they were opened and ready for inspection, before
departing.
To commomorate the opening of the Exhibition,
Prince Higashi Kuni entertained some 820 guests at a
banquet at the Municipal Auditorium on Sunday evening,
March 14.
Most of the prominent Japanese and
foreigners who were present at the opening ceremonies
at the Exhibition Grounds the same afternoon, were
also present at the banquet. The principal Japanese
and foreign guests were seated according to their
respective rank or importance at the main table with
Prince Higashi Kuni, with the Minister of The Nether-
lands, as the ranking foreign official present, at
the Prince's right.
At the reception preceding the
banquet the Dutoh Minister inquired from the secretary
representing the Frenoh Ambassador, and from me as
representing

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
representing the American Ambassador, as well as from
other foreign officials present, whether they would
wish him to speak on their behalf, as well as his own,
to which all agreed. Following a brief address of
thanks and appreciation by the Mayor, the Netherlands
Minister, for himself and on behalf of the foreign
missions represented, briefly, in French, thanked the
Prince for the invitation and for the opportunity of
joining in the ceremonies and celebration of the
opening of the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition,
for which all wished success.
A plan of the Exhibition Grounds with a numbered
directory of the various buildings, pavilions, et
cetera, is transmitted herewith.
A brief description
of the Exhibition, of the principal halls, and the
costs, and the countries taking part, is given on the
reverse side of the plan.
.The whole has been planned
along very modernistic lines, with ideas taken possibly
from the Century of Progress Exposition at Chicago a
few years ago, and is on the whole impressive and very
festive. A number of the more important buildings
are not yet completed or ready for visitors, and
several of the foreign exhibits have not yet arrived
or been put in place. However, all should be ready
in about two weeks, and will be quite worth visiting.
To see it all thoroughly would take perhaps two or
three days.
The Consulate is not informed of the extent of
American participation, although it is understood
that a number

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
that a number of private American exhibits have been
reoeived. (A report on this subject will be made
later when information may be obtained). It is
known, however, that an American circus troupe has
been engaged to give performances in the arena
where the opening ceremonies were held, and that a
huge model of the City of San Francisco, which was
last exhibited at the recent San Diego Exposition,
brought over personally by the artist and designer,
a Mr. R. G. Theurkauf, will be on display.
The city itself is in very festive attire.
Beginning in the large plaza in front of the huge
new railway station which was only recently opened,
and along the sidewalks of all thc principal streets
throughout the city, decorative, modernistic, vari-
colored pillars proclaim that the Exhibition is on.

SIR:
I have the honor to report that, on the occasion
of the visit of some 35 members (Japanese and Americans)
of the Japan-America Society of Tokyo, including
American Consul and Mrs. Arthur F. Tower and Mr. Gerald
Warner of the Embassy, the Mayor of Nagoya and the
Nagoya Chamber of Commorce and Industry, promoters of
the Nagoya Pan-Pacific Peace Exhibition, on April 24,
1937, fittingly held a so-called "America Day" at the
Exhibition.
With a large American flag prominently displayed
over the main entrance gate, and American and Japanese
flags crossed at the entrance to the pavilion for the
reception of guests and at other buildings to be
visited, the visitors from Tokyo were greeted on
arrival at the Exhibition by "peaceful" fireworks
from which more American and Japanese flags were
released, and each visitor was given flags and deco-
rated with colorful flag-bedges to mark him out among
the throng of other visitors at the Exhibition that
F/G
day.
Representative

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
一 8 -
Representative members of the local American
community assisted in receiving the visitors from Tokyo
and in conducting them around the Exhibition, with the
size and scope and general make up of which all seemed
very much impressed. Up to this time, so the Exhibi-
tion authorities stated, over two million persons have
already visited the Exhibition since its opening on
Maroh 15, 1937.
In the evening of the same day the Mayor and
other officials of the Kxhibition and prominent busi-
ness men of Nagoya, entertained the visitors from
Tokyo at a "Welcome Dinner". On this occasion the
President of the Nagoya Chamber of Commerce and
Industry delivered a brief address of welcome of which
the following is a translation:
"I feel it a distinot honor and pleasure to
greet you members of the Japan-American Society
of Tokyo here this evening on behalf of the
sponsors of this dinner party, namely, the Lichi
Prefecture, Nagoya City and Nagoya Chamber of
Commeree and Industry. We have to thank you
very mueh for your kind visit to this city and
for your thorough inspection of the Rxhibition
in spite of the pressure of your business. We
consider your visit not only a great honor but
also deeply significant in view of the fact that
it has, among its prime objects, the promotion
of international friendship.
"Our Exhibition is the first international
exposition held under public management in Japan.
It is enjoying good days continuously since its
opening on March 15th and already the mumber of
visitors has exceeded the 2 million mark. This
is in a large measure due to the friendly as-
sistance and cooperation given us by foreign
countries and cities. For instanse, San
Francisco has displayed a beautiful model of its
city, and Los Angeles, Dallas and other cities
have also their respective exhibits, each with
its representative feature.. To express our
appreciation

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
appreciation of their participation, we have
held the American Day at the Exposition which
you have just honored with your visit.
"The City of Nagoya has made striking
progress recently as an industrial city. It
has a population of over 1,l60,000 and with
Tokyo and Osaka, it is one of the most flourish-
ing commercial and industrial centers of Japan.
With this Exposition as a turning point, the
city will surely make great stridesas an inter-
national city and we firmly believe the relation
between this city and your country will become
closer hereafter. May we ask you to inspect
our city carefully to note the industrial growth
we are making.
"We feel very sorry that we cannot entertain
you properly due to the meagre arrangement but
hope you will enjoy yourselves as time permits
you.
"In closing this short address of welcome,
we wish you all good health and happiness".
To this address Consul Tower responded with the
following message:
"Your Excellencies, Ladies, and Gentlemen:
"His Exoellency the American Ambassador,
Mr. Joseph C. Grew, has asked me to express to
you his deep regret that other engagements have
prevented him from being present on this
occasion to participate with you in the cele-
bration of American Day of the Pan-Pacifio
Peace Exposition. He has requested me in his
behalf to assure you of his sympathy with the
motives which have prompted this undertaking and
his hope that the success achieved will surpass
the expectation of those who have contributed
their labor and support toward its realization.
"The name 'Pan-Pacifio Peace Exposition'
is particularly felicitous. Its choice to
describe this enterprise is proof that we are
becoming increasingly aware of the benefit
derived from a broader appreciation of the art,
the skill, and the industry of others.With
such appreciation must come an understanding
of the need to maintain an atmosphere so that
the things by which we live, as well as the
things for which

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.98)
things for which we live, may attain their
highest development. Consequently, the words
'Peace Exposition' are the expression not only
of purpose but of conviotion.
The City of Nagoya may well be proud of the
high repute or its products throughout the world.
We, as Americans, are proud to join with you in
celebration of the substantial achievements of
your City. We hope that through this busy gate-
way of neighborly intercourse in this Peaceful
Ocean will pass an ever growing stream of
travelers who come and go, bearing with them
their goods and their good will".
On the following day, before returning to Tokyo,
the visitors were further entertained by the Chamber

at a luncheon at the villa of Mr. Jirozaemon Ito,

growth of this city as a commercial and industrial

Past and Present
The metropolis of Middle Japan. as Nagova is someumes Called.
was some Bso years ago a tiny, scattered community, little known
except for the venerated Shinto hrine at At>uta which hd its origin
as far back as the sccond century A. D. The event which won for
Nagoya an cminent place on the map of Japan was the construction

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
of a castle in 1610 by Iyeyasu Tokugawa, founder of the Tokugawa
Shogunate, for his son, Yoshinao, to govern the district when the
former took the helm of the nation. This towering citadel, which
is still today a great landmark, was a conducive factor to the deve-
lopment of the community, as it offered security to the inhabitants.
The wide fertile plains around Nagoya were also instrumental to its
growth, for they assured the people rich agricultural yields. Thus,
this sparsely populated area grew up not long afterwards into a
sizable town of 55,ooo. By the close of the Tokugawa period in
1867, the population reached 130,000.
NAGOYACASTLE

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.94)
111111
MUNICIPAL OFFICE
With the advent of the Meiji Restoration in 1s6s which ushered
in constitutional monarchy to Japan, Nagoya veered from decorative
handicraft enterprises to modern industries. This was not hard to
predict, as the place is ideally located in a climati ally mild and
extensive area, with turbulent rivers nearby, suitable for hydro electric

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.93)
#
HIROKOJI-DORI, A BUSY THOROUGHFARE IN THE HEART OF NAGOYA
harnessing, and a sheltered ocean inlet adaptable for overseas trans-
portation at its very doorstep. These latent factors waited only for
an opportune day to come for their utilization.
Thereafter, in both scope and scale, the new industries of Nagoya
have kept growing at an amazing rate. As a necessary concomitant,

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.93)
BFEFFEEEEEEEERER
EEEFF
EEEEEFEFF
A NEW TOURIST HOTEL
the population has multiplied in a similar manner. These dynamic
developments are still proceeding uninterrupted. From a medioere
city, Nagoya has risen in a short span of half a century to the third
largest city and the second industrial centre of Japan.
Nagoya is, in some respects, still in the process of urbanization.

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
Larger and larger ferro-concrete structures are being added to those
already existing; more r ads constructed, widened, straightened or
paved to meet fully the requirements of the day. Spacious parks
have been layed out in numerous places, and sanitation works are
well provided. The movement to divide the city into ideal indust-
rial, commercial and residential zones is well under way.
A QUIET NOOK IN ONE OF NAGOYAS WELL-PLANNED PARK

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.97)
a
A POWERFUL STEAM ELECTRIC GENERATING PLANT
INDUSTRY
With the increased mechanization of industry, the problem of
electrification has come to assume a role of paraumount importance
in manufacturing centres. Nagoya has the good fortune of being
located adjacent to an area containing the greatest concentration of
hydro-electric power plants in Japan. Nine power lines from this
region supply the city with cheap and abundant clectricity. In
addition, there are two powerful steam turbine generators within the
city. Synchronization of these power lines, moreover, assures the city
of a constant and uniform supply of electricity even if one or more
of the power plants should break down.
Nagoya has the added advantage of possessing a spacious area
with ample room for expansion and being contiguous to a fertile

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
LOOMOTIVE WORKS
TENTILE MACHINERY WORKS
909
obtained will occupy no less than 40 9% of the national tigure.
The distriet of Nagoya is also the headquarters of the woolen
spinning and weaving industry in this country. About 250 million
yen worth of woolen fabries was produced here last year which
constituted about 75 9% of the nation's turnout. Factors which
have greatly contributed to the development of the textile industry,
in addition to the many favourable conditions already mentioned,
are suitable atmospheric moisture and abundant soft water which are
exceedingly helpful to textile manufacturing.
The engineering industry, in spite of its recent origin here, is

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.97)
A CORNER OF INDUSTRIAL NAGOYA
agricultural hinterland. Consequently, land prices are decidedly
lower and commodity prices generally cheaper here than in other
cities of Japan. These economie factors have greatly served to
stimulate the growth of the city.
The district of Nagoya has come to be recognized as one of the
great centres of the world's pottery, cotton and woolen industries.
IN
INTERIOR OF A COTTON MILL

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
ELECTRIC FANS IN THE MAKING
now the second enterprise of Nagoya. Among the many products
under this category, the most representative ones are aeroplanes,
locomotives, railroad coaches, spinning and weaving machinery,
machine tools, electrie motors and electrical apparatuses, and bicy-
cles. Lately automobiles have been added to this list. It should
be noted that the phenomenal growth made in the local textile
industry owes considerably to the economical supply of the most
efficient spinning machines and looms manufactured in this city.

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
7
BOTTLING BEER
Of all the industries, this group of engineering works is recording
the greatest rate of increase in production value and still has much
room for further expansion.
Third in value is the foodstuff and beverage industry. Prominent
items in this group are flour, " sake," beer, and canned and bottled
preservatives. In flour milling, Nagoya holds third place in Japan,
and in beer brewering, it is one of the four chief producing center
of the nation.

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
DECORATING
The pottery industry then follows in amount of production.
Japanese pottery making was first put on an industrial basis in the
district of Nagoya over 7oo years ago where, stimulated by a rich
and almost unlimited supply of raw pottery materials, it has flourished
unrivalled ever since. More than 70 % of the total pottery output of
Japan is produced here today. Although an extensive range of
pottery wares is manufactured in Nagoya, household wares occupy
about 95 % of the total production. Tablewares to appsal to the
most aristocratie taste as well as to suit the purse of the most humble
homes are successfully manufactured in Nagoya. Others under this
category are tiles, insulators and sanitary wares. These products are
finding larger markets abroad cach year.
FIRING

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
GLASSWARES
Closely allied to this group is the glassware industry which is
making marked progress in quality and quantity.
Cement, caustic sodas, bleaching powder, synthetic fertilizers, acids
and other chemicals are manufactures of the district carried on large
scale, chiefly to meet the requirement of the lome market, but there
is a growing tendeney for these products being exp rted.
Toys, especially wooden toys, are almost invariably manufactured
under family scale, but as the homes engaged in toy making are
very numerous, the aggregate value of the production amounts to
quite a substantial figure.

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
TESTING CLOCKS BEFORE SHIPPING THEM ABROAD
The clock industry of Japan had its origin in Nagoya about 50
years ago. It is not surprising to note, therefore, that the industry
should find the greatest development in this city. Musical instru-
ments, in particular, violins, are noted products of Nagoya.
The sight of huge and numerous log-jammed ponds around the
harbour district will instantly impress one of the importance of the
lumber industry in the city. The manufacturing industry of plywood
and wooden chest board here is, in fact, one of the largest of its
kind in this country.
Hardware manufactures, such as cast iron wares and enamel
wares, are also enterprises worthy of mention.
The world-renowned clois nne wares are virtually monopoly prod-
LACQUER WARES
PAPAR FANS

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.98)
ucts of Nagoya. True to their artistic nature, these beautiful articles
are not produced under a mass production basis. Delicately designed
lacquer wares, elegant paper fans and graceful lanterns are alsofold
and famed products of this district. There are many other small
and nondescript! enterprises which are too numerous to be mentioned
here.

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
THE NEW NAGOYA STATION
TRANSPORTATION, COMMUNICATION
AND COMMERCE
The first prerequisite for the development of commeree is undoubt-
edly good transportation facilities. In response to this basic require-
ment, huge investments have been unsparingly put into harbour,
canal and railway station projects in Nagoya. These created facilities
will form a coordinated system of land and ocean transportation,
capable of meeting most adequately the growing requirement of a
growing city.
A palatial new railway station to serve the 20 millions of passen-
gers who pass through here annually has been just built at a cost
of ¥ 25,000,000. It is today the largest and most modern railway
structure in Japan. This station is linked by three of the most im-
portant railway lines in Japan, namely, the Tokaido-sen which is by
far the greatest and oldest Jine in Japan, running between Tokyo
and Kobe; the Kwansai-sen running between Nagoya and Osaka via
Nara through a southern route; and the Chuo-sen running between
Tokyo and Nagoya through a mountainous, central route.

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
Internally, the city is covered with a network of trolley lines and
bus lines, and externally, efficient electrified railways radiate from
Nagoya to its suburban cities.
The completion in 193o of the Nakagawa Canal at a cost of
Y 1s,000,000 marked a great event in the history of Nagoya. The
canal extends through the southern industrial district of the city
and links Nagoya Railway Freight Station with the harbour. It is
8,210 metres long, 36 to 91 metres wide, and 3 metres deep, with
an annual capacity of handling 3.5 million tons of cargo. An out-
standing feature of the canal area is that the waterfronts are set
aside to the breadth of 50 metres as sites for landing places, ware-
houses and highways, and to a further breadth of 91 metres as sites
for industrial plants.
The sum of Y3s,000,o00 has been appropriated to cover expen-
ditures expected to be needed up to the completion of the 4th stage
harbour improvement work in 193s. The harbour will shortly have
ample accommodations to serve a fleet of 66 occan-going liners with
an aggregate tonnage of 33o,o0o tons. Already ships from all the
CANAL SCENE

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.97)
leading ports of the world are touching Nagoya as an important
port of call.
Aerial transportation, too, is being well provided. An international
airport was constructed on the reclaimed grounds of the barbour in
1934, and regular passenger and mail services were established to
connect Nagoya via air with the principal cities of Japan as well as
with those of the Asiatic continent.
Direct wireless communication with London, Paris, Berlin, Rome,
Warsaw, and Geneva became possible with the establishment of
wireless stations at the outskirts of the city in 192s. These distant
cities have thus become veritable next-door neighbours to Nagoya.
When the port of Nagoya was first opened to foreign commerce
in 1907, exports through the port in that year amounted to only
¥65,000 and imports ¥132,185. However, Nagoya's foreign trade
has witnessed a steady and rapid increase cach year with the con-
stant improvement of the port facilities. in 1936, the export figures
reached ¥131,500,961 and import Y 10s,777,074. Nagoya is now
ranked fourth as a trading port in Japan.
4
THE BUSY PORT OF NAGOYA

A NEWSPAPER CARRIER PLANE, MANUFACTURED IN THIS CITY.
WHICH MADE A SUCCESSFUL GOOD-WILL FLIGHT TO SIAM
Cotton textile is the largest export item of Nagoya. Better known,
however, is the poreelain ware export. In value, it ranks second,
but constitutes about 90% of the total porcelain ware exports from
Japan. Other leading exports mentioned in the order of their im-
portance are woolen textile, railway cars and locomotives, toys, looms,
plywoods and chest boards, and hardware.
Among the imports, wool stands far aloof as the most important
item. Pulp, lumber, coal, corn, cotton, oil cake, oil yielding seeds
and beans are other prominent imports.

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
SUNSET VIEW OF NAGOYA CASTLE
PLACES OF INTEREST IN NAGOYA
The Nagoya Castle
There are many feudal castles still existing in Japan, but the
castle of Nagoya execels them all in point of construction, preserva-
tion and beauty. After the Meiji Restoration in 1sos, the whole
castle precinct was converted into an Imperial Detached Palace. In
198o, it was granted to the city as an Imperial gifi for the inspet-
tion of the general public.
Though the castle has lost much of its past glamour, it is still the
first attraction of Nagoya. In spite of its 3 centuries of age, it stands
intact with an imposing dignity. Its white five-storied tower soars
majestically in symmetry with the gracefully curved foundation of
massive stoneworks upon which it rests to overlook a precinet of
deep moats, wide and clean gravel walks, and stubby pine groves.
The famous pair of gold dolphins, cach measuring over 8 feet high
and covered with scales of gold, are perehed aloft the topmost roof
of the tower. As they glisten dimly in the bright sunshine, they

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
remind one of weird
legendary creatures.
This seemingly extrav-
agant gesture of Iyeya-
su Tokugawa when he
ordered millions of yen
worth of gold be con-
verted into such gro-
tesque objects was very
likely partly for talis-
manic effect and partly
for a strategical display
of wealth and power to
would-be-invaders.
Another interesting
building lying within
the inner moat is the
palace quarter. Here,
for many generations
GOLD DOLPHINS
the descendants of the
Tokugawas had lived in feudal manner until the Meiji Restoration.
The Royal Room which was graciously occupied by Their Majes-
ties during their sojourn here has been preserved unaltered. The
palace is a veritable art gallery of 1'th Century Japan. It contains
numerous chambers partitioned by walls profusely ornamented with
beautiful mural paintings done in gold and silver, all painted by
masters of that period. There is a wide and long wooden corridor
flanking the chambers which emits a strange, squeaking sound when
treaded upon. The sound is said to have been intended to detect
the approach of stealthy intruders. Intricately carved wooden tran-
soms and elaborately decorated, standing screens are other alluring
objects of the building. To be in the midst of this environment is
to feel momentarily the cause of ana hronism in a ancient setting.
Perbaps with a little help of imagination, one can sense the atmos-
phere of the place as it was when feudalism was in flower in Japan.
No delineation can do justice to the castle. Only by secing it in
person can one fully appreciate its profound appeal and realize why
an old saying as the following is well-known throughout Japan : -
The Castle is the thing
Whereon hinges
The fame of Nagoya.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
ATSUTA SHRINE
Atsuta Shrine
This age-old Shinto Shrine is venerated by the whole nation be-
cause it enshrines the Sacred Sword which is one of the three treas-
ured heirlooms of the Imperial Family. The Shrine has been built
after the pattern of the Grand Shrine at Ise. There is nothing pre-
tentious about the Shrine, but in its quiet simplicity amidst a verdant
growth of centuries-old trees lies the inherent solemnity of the place.
A glimpse of religious Japan will be had in watching worshippers
offering ardent prayers at the Shrine.

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
Cloisonne Factory
For those who have admired cloisonne wares and have wondered
how these objects of art are made, a pleasant treat is in store for
them at the Ando Cloisonne shop. Here, visitors are welcome to
see the whole process of cloisonne manufacture. In a shop pervaded
with the scent of burning joss sticks, master craftsmen will be seen
squated in Japanese fashion, engrossed in their creative task; some
painting or enamelling with pigments as vari-coloured as the rainbow,
others retouching the designs with delicate precision, and still others
firing the wares or polishing the products with utmost patience.
After going through these scenes, one is sure to leave the shop with
a deeper appreciation of cloisonne wares than ever before.
LAYING SLVER BANDS ON A COPPER BASE IN CLOISONNE MANUFACTURING

A TYPICAL JAPANESE GARDEN IN THE PARK
Other places of interest in Nagoya
Tokugawa-en, a large, refined tea garden. Located here is the
recently built Tokugawa Museum which contains a priceless
collection of articles of historical and cultural value fiom the
Tokugawa family.
Tsurumai Ko-en, a well-planned park laid out on a motif com-
bining western and Japanese influences. It contains a large
city auditorium, a library, an art gallery, athletic and recrea-
tion grounds, an old translocated tea garden, and a graceful
edifice built after the Momoyama style of architecture.
THE CITY AUDITORIUM

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
SUNSET AT THE RHINE
INUYAMA CASTLE
river spreads into a rippling silvery expanse. Rising abruptly trom
the bank at this point is a precipitous pine-clad hill on which, like
a sentinel on vantage ground, stands the feudal castle of Inuyama.
Flat boats specially built to take in the thrilling sights of this kalei-
doscopie course can be had with expert boatmen to man these crafts
with oar and pole. In summer, cormorant fishing is another fasci-
nating attraction of the Nippon Rhine.
Gamagori
Hard by the southeast of Nagoya, beautiful Gamagori is attracting
foreign tourists in greater number cach year. It lies facing a tran-
quil sea with little hills at the back which harmonizes well with
the picturesque seashore setting. The climate is always mild and
healthful here. Its fame as one of the most beautiful all-year-round
resort in Central Japan is undoubtedly a deserving distinction. A
modern, de-lux hotel with a blending of occidental and oriental
tastes assures a comfortable sojourn at Gamagori for even the luxury-
accustomed tourists.

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
SEASHORE VIEW OF GAMAGORI
CHITA PENINSULA
Chita Peninsula
The peninsula extends south from Nagoya, forming a long bulging
arm from the mainland. Clean, sandy beaches which in summer
become popular bathing grounds compose a great portion of its
shoreline. Located here are sandy hills uniquely adaptable for skiing
the year round. The place is also rich in scenic beauty. A motor
drive round the peninsula should be an enjoyable trip well-worth
the time.
NAGOYA CHAMBER OF COMMERCE
AND INDUSTRY
Institutions known as either " Chamber of Commerce " or " Cham-
ber of Commerce and Industry" are, needless to say, found in
practically all the large cities of the world. Their forms of organi-
zation differ from country to country, ranging from government
supervised institutions to purely private associations. To give the
reader some idea of the Chamber institutions in Japan, a short
account of the Chamber of Nagoya at this closing space will not be
out of place.

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
Among its many functions, the Chamber endeavours to gather and
publish commercial and industrial data and statistics; make researches
on current economic problems: answer to inquiries made by the
Government relating to industry and commerce; file petitions with
the Government relating to similar problems : assist business transac-
tions, both local and foreign, by acting as a connecting link between
sellers and buyers ; mediate or arbitrate in commercial and industrial
disputes ; certify documents ; test and certify qualifications of pros-
pective employees : promote and participate in exhibitions and fairs;
lead and spnosor civic activities : and promote international under-
standing and good-will through private channels.

=== Page 205 === (confidence 1.00)
TRANSLATION
THE INTERNATIONAL EXPOSITION OF JAPAN
in celebration of the 2600th year
since the accession to the
throne of the first
Emperor Jimmu
Tokyo, March 23, 1938.
Mr. Ambassador:
I am sure that Your Excellency has already
learned that during the year 1940 a series of national
festivals of exceptional importance will be organized
with a view to commemorating and celebrating the 26o0th
anniversary of the Accession of the First Emperor Jimmu
to the throne of Japan. On that occasion a great
International Exposition will be held at Tokyo under
the distinguished auspices of his Imperial Highness
Prince Chichibu and with the support of the Imperial
Government.
This International Exposition, in which we are
inviting all foreign nations to participate, will
mark, we hope, an appreciable progress in the work of
drawing together the two civilizations, oriental and
occidental, and will thus contribute to the strengthening
of world peace.
As Direotor-General of the Exposition, I have
requested our Minister for Foreign Affairs to take the
necessary steps for convoking the governments of the
nations
His Excellency,
Joseph Clark Grew,
Ambassador Extraordinary and Plenipotentiary
of America.

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
2
nations friends of Japan. We are informed that the
letter of invitation has been already transmitted to
your government through the diplomatic channel.
I am convinced that the governments of the
foreign countries will be disposed to reply favorably
to our invitation. Nevertheless, I am taking the
liberty of requesting Your Excellenoy to be so good as
to urge your government to be represented and to partici-
pate offioially (in the Exposition).
I have the honor to transmit to Your Excellency
under separate cover the documents which I have had
drawn up to explain our aims and to set forth briefly
the project of the Exposition in question. In regard
to the abridged regulations, I shall send to Your Ex-
cellency shortly a revised edition thereof.
In expressing to Your Excellency in advance my
appreciation, I beg you, Mr. Ambassador, to accept the
assuranoes of my highest consideration.
(Signed)
G. Fujihara

Ambassador had promised in hie note
of invitation to supply. On April 1?

Department with a prospectus end other
documents relating to the Exposition.
The prospectus shows that the Exposi-
tion 1s to be under the direction of
an organization known as the Associs-
tion of the Internationel Exposition
of Japan, which has been formed by
ten representetive public and semi-
public bodies.

MAY 3-1938
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
U
An invitation to the Government of the United States
to participate in an international exposition to be held
at Tokyo, Japan in 1940 has been received from the
894.607 TOKY0/20
Japanese Government through the Japanese Ambassador.
It is the usual procedure of this Division to for-
ward copies of invitations to international expositions
to the different Government Departments with the request
that appropriate comments and recommendations be made
relative to the participation of this Government.
It is thought, however, that in this case it would
be advisable to establish the policy of the Department in
this regard before consulting the other Departments.
Appropriate participation on the part of the Government
would require a large appropriation which might be rather
diffioult to justify before the Congress. It should be
remarked, however, that the Japanese Government has
aocepted the invitations to participate in both the New
York
F
F
G

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.97)
-a
York World's Fair and the Golden Gate International
Exposition in San Franoisco in 1939, and, while our
records do not indicate the extent of their participation,
I have heard unofficially that the Japanese plans for
their exhibits call for an outlay of approximately
$750,000, of which $500,000 wil1 be spent at the Golden
Gate Exposition and $250,000 at the New York World's Fair.
In view of the fact that the Japanese Exposition
does not take place until May-August 1940, our requests of
the other Government Departments for their recommendations
might conveniently be delayed.
1S
Riohard Southgate
IC:RDM:MDF

Department of Foreign Affairs,
Tokyo, July 29, 1932.
Monsieur l'Ambassadeur:
I have the honor to inform Your Excellency that the
Koseikai(Association for the Study of Industrial Policy)
and the Dai Nihon Fujin Rengo Kai (Japanese Federation of
Womens Societies) are planning to hold a Women's and Chil-
dren's International Exhibition in Ueno Park, Tokyo, from
March 17 to May 10, 1933. As will be noted from the enclosed
pamphlet setting forth an outline of the Exhibition, pre-
parations are now proceeding apace.
The promoters are desirous of having at the Exhibition,
by way of comparison, similar material from foreign countries.
and in this connection, the Department of Education, which is
supporting the project, has asked that the competent authori--
ties of the United States be approached with a request to parti-
cipate in the Exhibition . I shall greatly appreciate any troubl
Your Excellency may take to realize the desire of the promoters
above alluded to.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to Your
Excellency the assurances of my highest consideration.
Count Yasuya Uchida
Minister for Foreign Affairs(SEAL)
His Excellency
Joseph C. Grew,
Ambassador Extraordinary and Plenipotentiary
of the United States of America,

INTRODUCTION TO THE WOMEN'S AND
CHILDREN'S INTERNATIONAL
EXHIBITION
The twentieth century holds a great deal in store
for the activity of women, and earnestly desires that
the firm establishment of lasting peace and happiness
throughout the world will be aohieved through their
understanding and oooperation. Japan has too many things
to be done on the part of women. On the one hand, 1t
is needless to state that the guidanee, eduoation, health,
hygienio conditione, eto. of children also hold the vital
1mportanee above all.
In view of sueh requirements of these times, this
international exhibition 1s organized with the essential
objeet of fostering commonsense and oultivating sense of
greater responsibility among women and ohildren. Further,
taking 1nto oonsideration the faots that the economic
polioy of the world in the past has attached too much
1mportance to production, (as a consequence of which
over-produetion has resulted, and whioh is the more im-
portant cause of world depression), and also that women
are ehiefly in charge of domestic economy, the Exhibition
a10

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.96)
-2
aims partioularly at the guidanoe and improvement of
practieal kmow1edge of domestic consumption.
Besides these, in this period of complex inter-
national relations, we shoul: take advantage of this
Exhibition, for furnishing an opportunity to exohange
those sinoere and respeotful sentiments between the
women and children of friendly countries and those of
Japan, es well as to exhibit referential materials
pertaining to food, olothing, homes, eduoation, health,
hygiene, household, furniture and utensils. There can
be no doubt of the great contribution such exhibition
may afford to the promotion of the mutual and good
foeling between nations, thus helping to build up the
road toward the peaoe, happiness and prosperity of the
world.
Reoently mations have recovered by degrees from the
wounds whieh the Great War inrlioted, while the dark
alouds whieh recently obsoured the sky in the Orient are
gradually fading away. All nations are endeavouring to
obviate of the distressing conditions whioh have lasted
so many years, and are looking hopefully forward the
prosperity in the fu ture. At this juneture, we undertake
th1s

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.96)
-8-
this exhibition, whioh is, we think, quite a timely
enterprise, and we feel oonfident that the slogan
"Brighter Japan; Ever Glorious Japan," will symbolize
the spring of the eighth year of the Showa Ere. It is
our convietion therefore that we can thus contribute
to the welfare of the nation, as also to the promotion
of a friondly understanding between the Treety owers
and Japan.
We earnestly hope that you, the government and the
public of their respective countries, in consideration
of the purposes of this international exhibition, will
unite in the support and assistance of this onterprise,
not only for the fulfilment of our ideals, but also for
the benefit of mankind at large.
Maroh, the 7th Year of Showa.
Count Hirotaro Hayashi, resident,

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.98)
INTRODUCTION TO THE WOMEN'S AND
CHILDREN'S INTERNATIONAL
EXHIBITION
The twentieth century holds a great deal in store for the
activity of women, and earnestly desires that the firm es-
tablishment of lasting peace and happiness throughout the
world will be achieved through their understanding and
cooperation. Japan has too many things to be done on the
part of women. On the one hand, it is needless to state
that the guidance, education, health, hygienic conditions,
etc. of children also hold the vital importance above all.
In view of such requirements of these times, this inter-
national exhibition is organized with the essential object of
fostering commonsense and cultivating sense of greater
responsibility among women and children. Further, taking
into consideration the facts that the economic policy of the
world in the past has attached too much importance to
production, (as a consequence of which over-production has
resulted, and which is the more important cause of world
depression), and also that women are chiefly in charge of
domestic economy, the Exhibition aims particularly at the
guidance and improvement of practical knowledge of do-
mestic consumption.
一5一

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
Besides these, in this period of complex international
relations, we should take advantage of this Exhibition, for
furnishing an opportunity to exchange those sincere and
respectful sentiments between the women and children of
friendly countries and those of Japan, as well as to exhibit
referential materials pertaining to food, clothing, homes,
education, health, hygiene, household, furniture and utensils.
There can be no doubt of the great contribution such
exhibition may afford to the promotion of the mutual and
good feeling between nations, thus helping to build up the
road toward the peace, happiness and prosperity of the
world.
Recently nations have recovered by degrees from the
founds which the Great War inflicted, while the dark clouds
which recently obscured the sky in the Orient are gradually
fading away. All nations are endeavouring to obviate of
the distressing conditions which have lasted so many years,
and are looking hopefully forward the prosperity in the
future. At this juncture, we undertake this exhibition,
which is, we think, quite a timely enterprise, and we feel
confident that the slogan "Brighter Japan; Ever Glorious
Japan,' will sympolize the spring of the eighth year of the
Showa Era. It is our conviction therefore that we can
thus contribute to the welfare of the nation, as also to the
promotion of a friendly understanding between the Treaty
Powers and Japan.
We earnestly hope that you, the government and the
一6一

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.98)
public of their respective countries, in consideration of the
purposes of this international exhibition, will unite in the
support and assistance of this enterprise, not only for the
fulfilment of our ideals, but also for the benifit of mankind
at large.
March, the 7th Year of Showa.
Count Hirotaro Hayashi, President,
THE WOMEN'S AND CHILDREN'S INTERNATIONAL
EXHIBITION
under the auspices of
KOSEIKAI (ASSOC. FOR THE STUDY OF INDUSTRIAL
POLICY, INC.)
DAI NIPPON RENGO FUJINKAI(THE JAPANESE FED.
OF WOMEN'S SOCIETIES.)

GENERAL SUGGESTIONS TO FOREIGN
COUNTRIES CONCERNING EXHIBITION
1. Request their respective governments, Red Cross Associ-
ations, orgaizations concerning education, social welfare,
women and children, as well as schools and institutions,
to exhibit characteristic articles pertaining to food-stuffs,
clothings, and housing, especially in regard to women
and children, and such articles as connected with educa-
tion, health, and hygiene, in addition to general houschold
articles, furnitures and utensils. Request them also to
display photographs, pictures and statistics, models.
samples, books, magazines, etc. in relation to the work
of the said organizations and institutions.
2. Request foreign countries also to exhibit dolls (showing
their national custumes), folksongs with musical notes,
their gramaphone records if possible and photos showing
life of women and children in their respective countries.
3. Request foreign countries to exhibit characteristic handi-
crafts, pea nt arts, and children's arts in their own
countries, cic
4. Request manufactures, or their unions, in foreign coun-
tries to display such goods for sale as consistent with
the above-mentioned purpose.
5. Reguest the rulers or ministers, men of authority, or
-8-

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
various organizations in foreign countries, to present
medals or trophies in honor of our women and children,
or for some specific purpose.
6. Request the organizations of women and children in
foreign countries to present a song with musical note
and record if possible, addressing to the women and
children of Japan.
7. Request the women and children of foreign countries
to present, in the name of the nation's women and children,
their national flag of ; meters in width to the women
and children of Japan.
8. Request that such exhibits as above mentioned will also
be presented to us where possible. They will be kept
permanently by certain museums, schools or other orga-
nizations after careful consideration of the Department
of Education, and the goodwill of those countries will be
commemorated for a long time to come.
9. Finally for those articles for sale in the Exhibition, the
exhibitor shall pay the expenses according to the rules
laid down by the exhibition authorities. As for those
exhibits not for sale, and if they are presented to this
country after exhibition, the expenses prescribed in the
regulations of this Exhibition shall not be charged.
Tojiro Kurahashi.
General Secretary,
THF WOMEN'S AND CHILDREN'S INTERNATIONAL
EXHIBITION.
